They have separate insurances, separate teaching schools, separate medical records, even. It's as if matters of the mouth are separate from the body. But of course, they're not. Every year, hundreds of thousands of Americans end up in emergency rooms for something like a toothache, toothache, something that could have been treated in a dental office. But most ERs don't have on-call dentists, so often these patients are sent home with painkillers and are told to go visit their dentist. But a lot of these people don't have dentists, or if they do, the cost for a visit is impossibly high. And yet people are held personally accountable for the state of their teeth in ways that they're not held accountable for many other health conditions. Poor dental care can be both life-threatening and humiliating, as if unhealthy teeth are a failure of individual responsibility rather than the symptom of a broken system. In Algodones, Dr. Magaña saw the state of dental care in America as an opportunity to turn this small Mexican border town into a place full of dental services catered to desperate Americans and Canadians. Looking for deep discounts. But the town was still mostly strip clubs and bars, and he felt like that was holding them back. So in 1980, Magaña became mayor, although the technically accurate term is municipal delegate. As municipal delegate, Magaña shuttered most of the cantinas. It was his first major step in transforming Algodones into Molar City. Year after year, the same buildings that housed those cantinas gave way to more clinics and pharmacies.

  p0: Well, it basically means that for any given project, and really for anything that you do in your life, a level of uncertainty exists.   p0: Full certainty puts those decisions into a different category. The things that we can be fully certain about. As developers, these things are few and far between. It's not bad news. And that's what we're talking about today. How to embrace this uncertainty.
  p0: Very often we try to sell certainty. This is such a common business practice. We want to sell a given product with all the features that you want for a fixed price and within a fixed timeline. All of these things mitigating uncertainty.
  p0: and ultimately viewing uncertainty as the enemy. But perhaps there's another way to do this. Perhaps we can embrace uncertainty. And instead of selling certainty, we sell change. Change is progressive.

There are two components that guide the allocation of the credits : preferences and set-asides. Preferences are outlined in the QAP and are used to score applicants’ projects, and set-asides are funds that are reserved every year from the allocation pool for specific types of projects.
  [1] The 2025 Low-Income Housing Tax Credit Qualified Allocation ...
To be eligible for an award of 9% Tax Credits , at least one Principal must have successfully developed, operated and maintained in compliance either one (1) 9% Tax Credit project in North Carolina or six (6) separate 9% Tax Credit projects totaling in excess of 200 units.
  [2] State Preemption of Local Equitable Housing Policies
More states participate in this process, known as preemption, every year in order to prevent cities from securing livable housing for all their constituents, making the affordability crisis worse for everyone .

  p0: this part was fairly straightforward one every night we take the branch and run our tests on it the execution always happens on the same dedicated machine to ensure that measurements remain as consistent as possible across different days two each test is executed 10 times in a row on the simulator while metrics are simultaneously sent to our internal technical database
  p0: 3. Since metric collection and reporting are implemented inside the application itself, C only needs to run the tests without any additional logic. 4. Thanks to this unified approach, we get full visibility into every step of the critical user path, exactly as it behaves in the production version. After the nightly run finishes, an N8N workflow starts at 10am and analyzes all collected startup metrics.
  p0: If any value exceeds our threshold, currently set to 4.5 seconds, the system immediately sends an alert to Slack. The biggest advantage is timing. We now discover regressions directly in, before release branching even starts. That gives engineers enough time to investigate and fix problems before regression testing begins.
  p0: We also maintain dashboards for long-term trend analysis and visual monitoring. An interesting side effect appeared when we migrated to Xcode 26. Startup times on simulators suddenly increased overnight. At first, we suspected a regression in our codebase. Later, we discovered that simulators in Xcode 26 had becomes lower overall.
  p0: The slowdown affected every stage of the startup flow, while real devices remained unchanged. Without historical performance tracking, identifying this would have been much harder. Why we intentionally slowed down the internet in C. It seemed like the problem was solved, performance tests were running every night, alerts were coming into Slack, and we could track trends in a dashboard. But at some point after another release, we again saw a regression in app launch time for users.
  p0: A natural question came up, why did the issue still reach production if we already had automated performance tests in place? This time, startup degradation only affected users with slow internet connections. The application relies heavily on request caching to reduce the time between launch and reaching the target screen. In one release, the cache mechanism broke silently, forcing the application to wait for fresh server responses before entering the UI.
  p0: On fast office Wi-Fi, the issue was almost invisible. For users with unstable or slow connections, startup time increased dramatically. After that incident, we redesigned the test execution flow. The new process works like this. 1. The first 10 runs execute under normal network conditions. 2. During these runs, the application fills local caches. 3. Then the network is artificially degraded, bandwidth limited to 400 kilobits per second.
  p0: Latency increased to 200 miz. 4. The same scenarios execute again with cached data already present on the device. To emulate poor network conditions, we use Network Link Conditioner. This tool throttles the entire device network stack instead of individual requests, which makes the environment much closer to real-world usage.
  p0: This approach gives us two important validations simultaneously. First, if a new unexpected network request appears in the critical path, startup time immediately increases. Second, we automatically verify cache correctness. If caching works properly, startup performance remains nearly identical even under slow network conditions. If the app suddenly waits for the backend again, the regression becomes obvious.
  p0: Recently, this setup caught a subtle but critical issue. One developer modified logic inside the caching layer. During code review, the mistake looked almost harmless. From the code itself, it was difficult to predict any major impact.

  p0: There's no special relationship between a parent and a child package, is there? No, just relationship on the file system. Yeah, so that's interesting, I think. Yeah, it's mostly just communicating. Like, you might be grouping things. Like, in the standard library, you see stuff in the encoding package or the...
  p0: The archive package is not a strict relationship, just it helps people to understand the groups. Yeah. And the way they're sub packages, you could say, but there's nothing enforced by the tools. Yeah. That's the term I usually use, sub package. It's easier than just, I don't know what else you call them. Yeah. They're semantic things, not syntactic things. It's just like, oh, this is, it's a sub package because we've decided it's a sub package, not because of where it sits necessarily.
  p0: One thing I'll touch on with what Chris was saying was, like, we don't have, there's, you know, there's some flexibility in Go, but I think a lot of package structure is really derived by the restriction of, you know, no circular dependencies. Like a lot of languages, like Ruby or Java, you can kind of go willy-nilly.
  p0: and put your packages anywhere or modules, whatever you want to call them. And, you know, at the end of the day, it's kind of bike shutting. It doesn't really matter. You know, there's nice groupings. Everyone has their own taste, but in Go, there's actually consequences to how you structure your packages and because of that circular dependency issue.
  p0: I think that's a really wonderful thing about Go. I think I reflect on Go a lot and it's, and it kind of subtly enforces a lot of sort of design lessons learned over the past, like, you know, 50 years. And people often don't appreciate that, right? Because for a long time I've been taught, you know, dependency should go one way, right? Like your handler can like depend on your domain, right?
  p0: but your domain shouldn't be coupled to HTTP, right? And that's just something that's been drilled into me since the start. But Go, I think, is the first language I've used where it's actually properly enforced that. It just won't let you do it. A lot of people get frustrated with that CERT codependency error, and you see lots of people asking questions about it. But to me, it's just a wonderful signal that there's something fishy in your design. You need to think about this and go, yeah, apply that rule. Dependencies should only go in one direction. Yeah.
  p0: Yeah, so tell us a little bit about that problem that happens with this. You know, you have these two packages and they are interacting in some way. Maybe you've got some interfaces so that they interact in a sort of nice decoupled way. But sometimes you find that one of them does then need to depend on the other and vice versa. And so if you have that, there's two packages and they both kind of need to depend on each other.
  p0: How do you solve that? Sure. Yeah. So, I mean, there's different takes on this in the community. I think that, you know, one common thing when you see two packages that need to depend on each other, people will kind of split off a third package to put the other thing in there. And then both those packages can depend on that one third package.
  p0: And then that kind of explodes over time. You keep doing that over and over again. And that's when you get a ton of packages. So for me, the way I structure things is I try to start from what I call the application domain. Just kind of like the things in your application that are, you know, they don't depend on technology. They're not related to your database or your web handlers or anything like that. It's just like your user, your customer, those kind of objects. I put those in the root directory. And then everything, all the other sub packages kind of reference that.
  p0: So your database might have a user database service where it can actually fetch users and communicate like that. And that can implement an interface inside of your domain package, your root package. And then your HTTP package, your web package, might then depend on your interface inside that root package. So you're not depending directly on the database, or your database isn't depending directly on your web handler.

  p0: frustrating working with any kind of machine learning than I do with regular coding. With regular coding, I can kind of recognize a specific output I'm trying to achieve. I can figure out the sequence of steps necessary to get there. And then I can kind of do one step at a time. And I'll have plenty of bugs. And I'll get frustrated when I don't know why something's not working. But I know that I can step through a debugger. I can check my assumptions. Eventually, I'll figure it out. Machine learning is not at all like that.
  p0: you won't necessarily even know if the thing you're trying to do is possible. And if it's not working, you can't just jump through a debugger and see which line of code is going wrong. You have to go through a kind of challenging, long, and rather vague kind of period of trying things out and trying to figure out why it's not training well. And that process requires a lot of tenacity, particularly because
  p0: For people with a coding background, it's going to be something you're not at all used to. So you kind of have to get familiar with learning and practicing some new skills that feel very different to what you're used to. What are the basic tools and techniques that people use in deep learning? Obviously, you need to start with some data. And while you're learning, you'll often be using data sets that are freely downloadable. In our course, we show you how to create your own data sets. So you need some data from somewhere.
  p0: To make it most interesting, ideally that data represents some actual project you're interested in solving.
  p0: So for example, one of our students I think was a sound engineer and they started with a corpus of environmental sounds that they wanted to classify. One of our students was a genomics scientist and so she started with a bunch of genomics data that she wanted to analyze. So you need some data. The first kind of part of your process is going to involve munging that data into a form ready for analysis. One of the nice things about deep learning is that process is generally much
  p0: shorter and simpler than older machine learning methods like linear regression or something like that. Because with neural networks, they can generally handle data in a pretty raw form.
  p0: So that munching process, most people tend to use Python for that, using libraries like Pandas in particular, also using an SQL database can be a really handy tool in that process. Once you've got the data in the form that you need it, you will need a library for...
  p0: creating or using a predefined architecture and feeding batches of data into that architecture and almost certainly doing that specifically on a GPU. The two most popular libraries that people use for this part of the process are called TensorFlow and PyTorch, coming from Google and Facebook, respectively.
  p0: They're more similar than they are different. And which one you choose will probably depend on what your colleagues and so forth are using. If you have no particular reason to choose one over the other, we recommend PyTorch because we find it's faster to develop with and easier to debug. But it's similar enough that I wouldn't mind too much at that point. But the main thing I would say is TensorFlow and PyTorch, neither of them are
  p0: convenient to work with directly. They're a little bit like playing around with C or Assembler versus writing Python. They're kind of more foundational things. And so in practice, the vast majority of people, whether they be students or practitioners or researchers, work with higher level APIs.

To be eligible for an award of 9% Tax Credits , at least one Principal must have successfully developed, operated and maintained in compliance either one (1) 9% Tax Credit project in North Carolina or six (6) separate 9% Tax Credit projects totaling in excess of 200 units.
  [3] Does Rent Control Work? What the Research Shows - LegalClarity
March 6, 2026 - Rent Control and the Supply of Affordable Housing In other words, rent control may redistribute the existing housing stock toward lower-income tenants even as it shrinks the total pie. Developers don’t stop building entirely, but they redirect. New construction in rent-controlled markets tends to skew toward luxury apartments and commercial properties that sit outside the regulatory framework. This makes economic sense for builders whose financing depends on projected returns, but it does nothing to increase the supply of moderately priced housing that most renters actually need.

  [0] Elements of Effective State Qualified Allocation Plans
There are two components that guide the allocation of the credits : preferences and set-asides. Preferences are outlined in the QAP and are used to score applicants’ projects, and set-asides are funds that are reserved every year from the allocation pool for specific types of projects.
